[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF30.0, F31.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess what brought me in is I’ve been kind of… I don’t know, ramped up around this park project idea. I work landscaping, usually I’m just a crew guy, you know, doing what needs doing, but lately I went from that to trying to run like three different bids at once, calling city people, trying to line up plant suppliers, and messaging pretty much everybody I know about this idea for a park.\n\nAnd it wasn’t even like I sat down and planned it in a normal way. It was more like once it got in my head, I couldn’t shut it off. I kept thinking of another person to call, another angle, another thing to price out, another message to send. It just got bigger and bigger, and I was acting like I was already in charge of the whole thing, when really I’m not usually that guy. That’s why I figured maybe I should talk to somebody, because it felt like it kind of took over.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel unusually happy or excited during this period of intense focus on the park project?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. It wasn’t like normal happy, though, like “oh, I’m having a good day.” It was more like I felt fired up, like I was suddenly seeing everything clearly and I was bigger than myself somehow, like I could push this whole thing through if people would just get out of the way and let me work.\n\nAt first it felt exciting, honestly. I was walking around thinking, “Why hasn’t anyone done this already? I can make this happen.” But then if someone questioned me, like a coworker saying the plan didn’t make sense or telling me to slow down, I’d get irritated really fast. Like way too fast. I’d snap at him harder than the situation called for, and afterward part of me knew it was too much, but in the moment it felt like they were blocking something important. So yeah, there was excitement, but it had this edge to it too.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about the park project?\n\nPatient: It was… about two weeks, I think. Not just like one excited day or a weekend where I got caught up in something. It started when I kind of began trying to remake myself for this park pitch, if that makes sense—like suddenly I wasn’t just showing up to work and doing landscaping, I was acting like I had to become the guy who could sell the whole thing and push it through.\n\nThat fired-up, overly confident feeling stayed pretty steady for around two weeks before I kind of crashed out of it. During that stretch it felt like I was just going, going, going with the idea, like everything was connected to it. Then after those couple weeks it wasn’t the same anymore. It was like all that certainty and energy just dropped off.\n\nDoctor: After you crashed out of that fired-up, overly confident feeling, did you feel sad, empty, or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t want to say it was like I had some clear, named feeling right away, like “now I’m depressed.” It was more like the air went out of me, if that makes sense.\n\nAfter being so keyed up and convinced this whole park thing was going somewhere, it felt strange and kind of flat when that dropped off. But I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly. Empty maybe? I don’t know. I mostly remember feeling kind of confused and worn down, like I’d been running on something and then suddenly it wasn’t there anymore.\n\nI guess I noticed I wasn’t excited anymore, and that felt bad in its own way. But if you’re asking whether I was crying all the time or had this obvious heavy depression feeling, I don’t know that I can say that for sure. It was more like… quiet, and uncomfortable, and I didn’t really know what to do with myself after being so wrapped up in it.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a period of time after the park project idea wasn't exciting anymore where you felt consistently sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I think if I’m being honest, it wasn’t just a couple bad days after the park thing stopped feeling exciting. That crash kind of landed on top of something that had already been hanging around for a long time.\n\nI’d say the depressed feeling has been over me for about a year now, like this weight in the background, but after the park project excitement dropped off, it got worse. This latest crash is probably the worst it’s gotten. It’s hard to separate it neatly, because I keep thinking, “Was I already depressed before that, and then I just got distracted by the project?” But yeah, after it wasn’t exciting anymore, I felt consistently low, and it didn’t really lift.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt abnormally happy or irritable and had increased energy, like with the park project, but without any clear reason or goal?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think if there was no clear reason or goal… because with the park project, there was definitely a thing I was aiming at, you know? It wasn’t random in my mind. It felt like I had this purpose and I was building everything around it.\n\nBut the feeling itself, before I crashed, did feel kind of abnormal for me. I was fired up in this way that was more than just being motivated. It was like I felt almost bigger than myself, like I had more force behind me than I usually do. And it wasn’t just happy exactly—it could turn sharp really fast.\n\nLike if a coworker questioned part of my plan, or even just said something like maybe I should slow down or think it through more, I’d snap at him way harder than made sense. In the moment it felt like they were blocking something important, but looking back, the reaction was out of proportion. I’d get irritated fast, like, “Why can’t you see this?” even if they were probably just trying to be reasonable.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’ve had that same thing with no reason at all, but I have had that fired-up, almost inflated feeling, and the irritability came with it pretty strongly.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt this abnormally happy or irritable with increased energy?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, probably more than once, though the park project is the one that stands out the clearest because it got so wrapped up in an actual plan.\n\nBut I’ve had that same kind of feeling before, where I’m not just in a good mood — it’s more like I’m fired up, like I’ve got this extra push in me, almost like I’m bigger than myself for a while. And when I’m in it, it doesn’t feel strange at first. It feels like, “Oh, this is what I’m supposed to be doing,” or “I finally have energy.” But then the way I react to people is what makes me look back and think it wasn’t normal.\n\nLike if someone at work questioned me, or said I should slow down, or just didn’t immediately get on board with what I was saying, I’d snap at them way harder than made sense. In my head at the time, it felt justified, like they were in the way or not understanding something obvious. But afterward I could see I was being too intense. So yes, I’d say there’s been more than one period like that, with that fired-up energy and irritability.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these periods of feeling fired up and then depressed seem to happen in a repeating pattern over time?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to look back and see if there’s, like, a rhythm to it, but it’s hard for me to tell. When I’m in the fired-up part, I don’t really think, “Oh, this is one of those times again.” It feels more like I’ve finally gotten myself together or I finally have energy and direction. Then when I drop, it feels like everything just catches up with me, and I start questioning whether I was ever doing okay in the first place.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve noticed it’s happened more than once, the getting really intense and then later feeling low, but I don’t know if I can say it’s a repeating pattern in a clean way. It’s not like I could point to a calendar and say, “Every few months this happens.” It’s more messy than that. I only really see it looking backward, and even then I’m not sure if I’m connecting dots that are actually there or just trying to make sense of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these periods of feeling fired up and then depressed happen in a cyclical pattern over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it cyclical exactly. I mean, I get what you’re asking, like whether it comes around in some kind of cycle or pattern, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed it that clearly.\n\nIt’s more like… when I look back, I can see there have been times where I was really fired up, really intense, and then later I’d be down. But in the moment it doesn’t feel like, “Here we go again, this is the cycle starting.” It feels like whatever is happening right then is just because of my life or my work or something I’m focused on.\n\nAnd then after I’m low, I start trying to piece it together, but it’s messy. I don’t know if I’m seeing an actual pattern or if I’m just trying to make sense of it after the fact. So I guess my answer is… maybe, but I haven’t been able to track it well enough to say yes for sure.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel like you were able to function normally or even better than normal during those periods when you were fired up, despite not sleeping much or needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… at the time I actually thought I was functioning better than normal. Like, that was part of what made it hard to see it as a problem, because I wasn’t just lying around or acting useless. I was getting things done — or at least I felt like I was.\n\nDuring that park project, I’d be up before daylight, already loading tools, thinking through what needed to happen that day, sketching out planting beds, looking up prices for native plants, trying to figure out what would work where. And then I’d go do a full day outside, physical work, and instead of coming home wiped out like I normally would, I still felt like I had to keep going. Like there was more to plan, more to fix, more to push forward.\n\nSo yeah, in my head it felt like I didn’t need as much sleep because I had all this momentum. I remember thinking, “This is good, I’m finally productive.” But looking back, it was kind of like I couldn’t shut it off. Even after working all day, I didn’t feel done. I felt driven, like stopping would mean I was wasting something.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of feeling fired up or abnormally happy that lasted for less than a week?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, less than a week… that’s hard for me to pin down, because I don’t usually mark the start and end of it clearly when it’s happening. It’s not like I wake up and think, “Okay, this is day one of being fired up.” It’s more gradual, or at least it feels that way to me.\n\nThere have been times where I felt really energized or unusually good for a few days, I think, but I don’t know if I can say for sure how long they lasted. Sometimes I only notice afterward that I was acting different or running on more energy than usual. And by then the days kind of blur together, especially if I was busy.\n\nSo… maybe? But I don’t feel confident giving you a clean yes or no. I’d probably need help figuring out what counts and how to track it, because my memory of the timing isn’t very exact.\n\nDoctor: When you feel fired up like you did with the park project, do you think of it as a distinct episode or just a part of your normal variation in mood?\n\nPatient: I guess at the time I treated it like it was just me being “on,” like maybe this was my normal motivated side finally showing up. I didn’t think, “This is a distinct thing happening to me.” I thought I was just locked in.\n\nBut looking back at the park project, it does feel separate from my usual mood. Before I crashed, I wasn’t just in a good mood or working hard. I felt fired up in this really intense way, almost like I was bigger than myself, like I could see exactly how everything should be done and I just needed everyone else to keep up.\n\nAnd that’s where it got weird, because if a coworker questioned my plan or said, “Maybe we should slow down,” I’d snap at him way harder than made sense. Like instantly irritated, almost offended, as if they were getting in the way of something important. At the time I would’ve said they were just being negative or lazy, but now I can see my reaction was too much.\n\nSo… I don’t know. In the moment it felt like normal variation, just being driven. But when I compare it to how I usually am, it does seem more like a distinct episode or state or whatever you’d call it.\n\nDoctor: When you're not feeling fired up, do you think you're a different person compared to when you are in one of those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… honestly, I do feel like a different version of myself. Not like I’m literally a different person, but the way I think and react is different enough that when I look back on it, it almost doesn’t feel like my normal self.\n\nWhen I’m not fired up, I can usually hear someone out, or at least I’m not so quick to take it personally. Like if a coworker says, “Maybe we should slow down,” or questions part of a plan, I might get annoyed, but I can usually pause and think about it.\n\nBut during that park project, before I crashed, I felt so fired up and almost… bigger than myself, I guess. Like I had this certainty that I knew what needed to happen and everyone else just needed to get on board. And if someone questioned me, I’d snap at him way harder than made sense. It wasn’t just irritation — it felt like they were blocking something important, and I’d react like it was some huge offense.\n\nThen later, when I’m back to normal, I’ll think, “Why did I talk to him like that?” Because that’s not really how I want to be. So yeah, I guess it does feel like I become a more intense, impatient version of myself.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling fired up, do you think it's something that happens to you, or is it just your normal self being more motivated?\n\nPatient: I think at the time it feels like my normal self, just more motivated, like I’m finally getting things done the way I should. I don’t experience it as, “Oh, something is happening to me,” while I’m in it. It feels more like I’m right, I’m clear, I have energy, and everyone else is dragging their feet.\n\nBut looking back, especially with the park project, it really doesn’t feel like just normal motivation. Before I crashed, I felt fired up in this intense way, almost bigger than myself, like I had this strong sense that I knew exactly what the plan should be. And if a coworker questioned that plan or told me we should slow down, I’d snap at him way harder than made sense.\n\nThat’s the part that makes me think maybe it’s not just me being motivated. Because normal motivated me might be focused or a little impatient, but I don’t usually react like someone is personally attacking me just because they suggest we take a step back. In that state, it feels like they’re blocking something important, and I get irritated fast. Then later I can see it was too much. So… in the moment it feels like me, but afterward it feels like something kind of took over or pushed me past my normal self.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your periods of feeling fired up and then depressed are connected, or are they separate events?\n\nPatient: I think they’re connected, yeah. At least with the park pitch thing, it didn’t feel like two totally random events. The fired-up part started when I began trying to sort of remake myself for that pitch — like I was pushing myself into this bigger, more confident version of me — and that whole stretch went on for about two weeks.\n\nIt wasn’t like one good afternoon and then I got sad for unrelated reasons. It was more like I kept building and building in that overconfident, fired-up state, and then after about two weeks I crashed. So looking back, it feels like part of the same cycle, even though when I was in it, I didn’t really see it that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’ve been trying to look back and see if there’s, like, a rhythm to it, but it’s hard for me to tell. When I’m in the fired-up part, I don’t really think, “Oh, this is one of those times again.” It feels more like I’ve finally gotten myself together or I finally have energy and direction. Then when I drop, it feels like everything just catches up with me, and I start questioning whether I was ever doing okay in the first place.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve noticed it’s happened more than once, the getting really intense and then later feeling low, but I don’t know if I can say it’s a repeating pattern in a clean way. It’s not like I could point to a calendar and say, “Every few months this happens.” It’s more messy than that. I only really see it looking backward, and even then I’m not sure if I’m connecting dots that are actually there or just trying to make sense of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these periods of feeling fired up and then depressed happen in a cyclical pattern over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it cyclical exactly. I mean, I get what you’re asking, like whether it comes around in some kind of cycle or pattern, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed it that clearly.\n\nIt’s more like… when I look back, I can see there have been times where I was really fired up, really intense, and then later I’d be down. But in the moment it doesn’t feel like, “Here we go again, this is the cycle starting.” It feels like whatever is happening right then is just because of my life or my work or something I’m focused on.\n\nAnd then after I’m low, I start trying to piece it together, but it’s messy. I don’t know if I’m seeing an actual pattern or if I’m just trying to make sense of it after the fact. So I guess my answer is… maybe, but I haven’t been able to track it well enough to say yes for sure.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel like you were able to function normally or even better than normal during those periods when you were fired up, despite not sleeping much or needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… at the time I actually thought I was functioning better than normal. Like, that was part of what made it hard to see it as a problem, because I wasn’t just lying around or acting useless. I was getting things done — or at least I felt like I was.\n\nDuring that park project, I’d be up before daylight, already loading tools, thinking through what needed to happen that day, sketching out planting beds, looking up prices for native plants, trying to figure out what would work where. And then I’d go do a full day outside, physical work, and instead of coming home wiped out like I normally would, I still felt like I had to keep going. Like there was more to plan, more to fix, more to push forward.\n\nSo yeah, in my head it felt like I didn’t need as much sleep because I had all this momentum. I remember thinking, “This is good, I’m finally productive.” But looking back, it was kind of like I couldn’t shut it off. Even after working all day, I didn’t feel done. I felt driven, like stopping would mean I was wasting something.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of feeling fired up or abnormally happy that lasted for less than a week?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, less than a week… that’s hard for me to pin down, because I don’t usually mark the start and end of it clearly when it’s happening. It’s not like I wake up and think, “Okay, this is day one of being fired up.” It’s more gradual, or at least it feels that way to me.\n\nThere have been times where I felt really energized or unusually good for a few days, I think, but I don’t know if I can say for sure how long they lasted. Sometimes I only notice afterward that I was acting different or running on more energy than usual. And by then the days kind of blur together, especially if I was busy.\n\nSo… maybe? But I don’t feel confident giving you a clean yes or no. I’d probably need help figuring out what counts and how to track it, because my memory of the timing isn’t very exact.\n\nDoctor: When you feel fired up like you did with the park project, do you think of it as a distinct episode or just a part of your normal variation in mood?\n\nPatient: I guess at the time I treated it like it was just me being “on,” like maybe this was my normal motivated side finally showing up. I didn’t think, “This is a distinct thing happening to me.” I thought I was just locked in.\n\nBut looking back at the park project, it does feel separate from my usual mood. Before I crashed, I wasn’t just in a good mood or working hard. I felt fired up in this really intense way, almost like I was bigger than myself, like I could see exactly how everything should be done and I just needed everyone else to keep up.\n\nAnd that’s where it got weird, because if a coworker questioned my plan or said, “Maybe we should slow down,” I’d snap at him way harder than made sense. Like instantly irritated, almost offended, as if they were getting in the way of something important. At the time I would’ve said they were just being negative or lazy, but now I can see my reaction was too much.\n\nSo… I don’t know. In the moment it felt like normal variation, just being driven. But when I compare it to how I usually am, it does seem more like a distinct episode or state or whatever you’d call it.\n\nDoctor: When you're not feeling fired up, do you think you're a different person compared to when you are in one of those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… honestly, I do feel like a different version of myself. Not like I’m literally a different person, but the way I think and react is different enough that when I look back on it, it almost doesn’t feel like my normal self.\n\nWhen I’m not fired up, I can usually hear someone out, or at least I’m not so quick to take it personally. Like if a coworker says, “Maybe we should slow down,” or questions part of a plan, I might get annoyed, but I can usually pause and think about it.\n\nBut during that park project, before I crashed, I felt so fired up and almost… bigger than myself, I guess. Like I had this certainty that I knew what needed to happen and everyone else just needed to get on board. And if someone questioned me, I’d snap at him way harder than made sense. It wasn’t just irritation — it felt like they were blocking something important, and I’d react like it was some huge offense.\n\nThen later, when I’m back to normal, I’ll think, “Why did I talk to him like that?” Because that’s not really how I want to be. So yeah, I guess it does feel like I become a more intense, impatient version of myself.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling fired up, do you think it's something that happens to you, or is it just your normal self being more motivated?\n\nPatient: I think at the time it feels like my normal self, just more motivated, like I’m finally getting things done the way I should. I don’t experience it as, “Oh, something is happening to me,” while I’m in it. It feels more like I’m right, I’m clear, I have energy, and everyone else is dragging their feet.\n\nBut looking back, especially with the park project, it really doesn’t feel like just normal motivation. Before I crashed, I felt fired up in this intense way, almost bigger than myself, like I had this strong sense that I knew exactly what the plan should be. And if a coworker questioned that plan or told me we should slow down, I’d snap at him way harder than made sense.\n\nThat’s the part that makes me think maybe it’s not just me being motivated. Because normal motivated me might be focused or a little impatient, but I don’t usually react like someone is personally attacking me just because they suggest we take a step back. In that state, it feels like they’re blocking something important, and I get irritated fast. Then later I can see it was too much. So… in the moment it feels like me, but afterward it feels like something kind of took over or pushed me past my normal self.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your periods of feeling fired up and then depressed are connected, or are they separate events?\n\nPatient: I think they’re connected, yeah. At least with the park pitch thing, it didn’t feel like two totally random events. The fired-up part started when I began trying to sort of remake myself for that pitch — like I was pushing myself into this bigger, more confident version of me — and that whole stretch went on for about two weeks.\n\nIt wasn’t like one good afternoon and then I got sad for unrelated reasons. It was more like I kept building and building in that overconfident, fired-up state, and then after about two weeks I crashed. So looking back, it feels like part of the same cycle, even though when I was in it, I didn’t really see it that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "Return the JSON now."

I work landscaping, usually I’m just a crew guy, you know, doing what needs doing, but lately I went from that to trying to run like three different bids at once, calling city people, trying to line up plant suppliers, and messaging pretty much everybody I know about this idea for a park.\n\nAnd it wasn’t even like I sat down and planned it in a normal way. It was more like once it got in my head, I couldn’t shut it off. I kept thinking of another person to call, another angle, another thing to price out, another message to send. I was walking around thinking, “Why hasn’t anyone done this already? I’d snap at him harder than the situation called for, and afterward part of me knew it was too much, but in the moment it felt like they were blocking something important. Not just like one excited day or a weekend where I got caught up in something. It started when I kind of began trying to remake myself for this park pitch, if that makes sense—like suddenly I wasn’t just showing up to work and doing landscaping, I was acting like I had to become the guy who could sell the whole thing and push it through.\n\nThat fired-up, overly confident feeling stayed pretty steady for around two weeks before I kind of crashed out of it. During that stretch it felt like I was just going, going, going with the idea, like everything was connected to it. Then after those couple weeks it wasn’t the same anymore. It was like all that certainty and energy just dropped off.\n\nDoctor: After you crashed out of that fired-up, overly confident feeling, did you feel sad, empty, or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t want to say it was like I had some clear, named feeling right away, like “now I’m depressed.” It was more like the air went out of me, if that makes sense.\n\nAfter being so keyed up and convinced this whole park thing was going somewhere, it felt strange and kind of flat when that dropped off. But I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly. Empty maybe? I don’t know. I mostly remember feeling kind of confused and worn down, like I’d been running on something and then suddenly it wasn’t there anymore.\n\nI guess I noticed I wasn’t excited anymore, and that felt bad in its own way. It was more like… quiet, and uncomfortable, and I didn’t really know what to do with myself after being so wrapped up in it.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a period of time after the park project idea wasn't exciting anymore where you felt consistently sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I think if I’m being honest, it wasn’t just a couple bad days after the park thing stopped feeling exciting. That crash kind of landed on top of something that had already been hanging around for a long time.\n\nI’d say the depressed feeling has been over me for about a year now, like this weight in the background, but after the park project excitement dropped off, it got worse. This latest crash is probably the worst it’s gotten. It’s hard to separate it neatly, because I keep thinking, “Was I already depressed before that, and then I just got distracted by the project?” But yeah, after it wasn’t exciting anymore, I felt consistently low, and it didn’t really lift.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt abnormally happy or irritable and had increased energy, like with the park project, but without any clear reason or goal?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think if there was no clear reason or goal… because with the park project, there was definitely a thing I was aiming at, you know? It wasn’t random in my mind. It felt like I had this purpose and I was building everything around it.\n\nBut the feeling itself, before I crashed, did feel kind of abnormal for me. I was fired up in this way that was more than just being motivated. It was like I felt almost bigger than myself, like I had more force behind me than I usually do. And it wasn’t just happy exactly—it could turn sharp really fast.\n\nLike if a coworker questioned part of my plan, or even just said something like maybe I should slow down or think it through more, I’d snap at him way harder than made sense. In the moment it felt like they were blocking something important, but looking back, the reaction was out of proportion. I’d get irritated fast, like, “Why can’t you see this?” even if they were probably just trying to be reasonable.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’ve had that same thing with no reason at all, but I have had that fired-up, almost inflated feeling, and the irritability came with it pretty strongly.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt this abnormally happy or irritable with increased energy?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, probably more than once, though the park project is the one that stands out the clearest because it got so wrapped up in an actual plan.\n\nBut I’ve had that same kind of feeling before, where I’m not just in a good mood — it’s more like I’m fired up, like I’ve got this extra push in me, almost like I’m bigger than myself for a while. And when I’m in it, it doesn’t feel strange at first. It feels like, “Oh, this is what I’m supposed to be doing,” or “I finally have energy.” But then the way I react to people is what makes me look back and think it wasn’t normal.\n\nLike if someone at work questioned me, or said I should slow down, or just didn’t immediately get on board with what I was saying, I’d snap at them way harder than made sense. In my head at the time, it felt justified, like they were in the way or not understanding something obvious. But afterward I could see I was being too intense. So yes, I’d say there’s been more than one period like that, with that fired-up energy and irritability.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these periods of feeling fired up and then depressed seem to happen in a repeating pattern over time?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to look back and see if there’s, like, a rhythm to it, but it’s hard for me to tell. When I’m in the fired-up part, I don’t really think, “Oh, this is one of those times again.” It feels more like I’ve finally gotten myself together or I finally have energy and direction. Then when I drop, it feels like everything just catches up with me, and I start questioning whether I was ever doing okay in the first place.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve noticed it’s happened more than once, the getting really intense and then later feeling low, but I don’t know if I can say it’s a repeating pattern in a clean way. It’s not like I could point to a calendar and say, “Every few months this happens.” It’s more messy than that. I only really see it looking backward, and even then I’m not sure if I’m connecting dots that are actually there or just trying to make sense of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these periods of feeling fired up and then depressed happen in a cyclical pattern over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it cyclical exactly. I mean, I get what you’re asking, like whether it comes around in some kind of cycle or pattern, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed it that clearly.\n\nIt’s more like… when I look back, I can see there have been times where I was really fired up, really intense, and then later I’d be down. But in the moment it doesn’t feel like, “Here we go again, this is the cycle starting.” It feels like whatever is happening right then is just because of my life or my work or something I’m focused on.\n\nAnd then after I’m low, I start trying to piece it together, but it’s messy. I don’t know if I’m seeing an actual pattern or if I’m just trying to make sense of it after the fact. So I guess my answer is… maybe, but I haven’t been able to track it well enough to say yes for sure.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel like you were able to function normally or even better than normal during those periods when you were fired up, despite not sleeping much or needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… at the time I actually thought I was functioning better than normal. Like, that was part of what made it hard to see it as a problem, because I wasn’t just lying around or acting useless. I was getting things done — or at least I felt like I was.\n\nDuring that park project, I’d be up before daylight, already loading tools, thinking through what needed to happen that day, sketching out planting beds, looking up prices for native plants, trying to figure out what would work where. And then I’d go do a full day outside, physical work, and instead of coming home wiped out like I normally would, I still felt like I had to keep going. Like there was more to plan, more to fix, more to push forward.\n\nSo yeah, in my head it felt like I didn’t need as much sleep because I had all this momentum. I remember thinking, “This is good, I’m finally productive.” But looking back, it was kind of like I couldn’t shut it off. Even after working all day, I didn’t feel done. I felt driven, like stopping would mean I was wasting something.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of feeling fired up or abnormally happy that lasted for less than a week?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, less than a week… that’s hard for me to pin down, because I don’t usually mark the start and end of it clearly when it’s happening. It’s not like I wake up and think, “Okay, this is day one of being fired up.” It’s more gradual, or at least it feels that way to me.\n\nThere have been times where I felt really energized or unusually good for a few days, I think, but I don’t know if I can say for sure how long they lasted. Sometimes I only notice afterward that I was acting different or running on more energy than usual. And by then the days kind of blur together, especially if I was busy.\n\nSo… maybe? But I don’t feel confident giving you a clean yes or no. I’d probably need help figuring out what counts and how to track it, because my memory of the timing isn’t very exact.\n\nDoctor: When you feel fired up like you did with the park project, do you think of it as a distinct episode or just a part of your normal variation in mood?\n\nPatient: I guess at the time I treated it like it was just me being “on,” like maybe this was my normal motivated side finally showing up. I didn’t think, “This is a distinct thing happening to me.” I thought I was just locked in.\n\nBut looking back at the park project, it does feel separate from my usual mood. Before I crashed, I wasn’t just in a good mood or working hard. I felt fired up in this really intense way, almost like I was bigger than myself, like I could see exactly how everything should be done and I just needed everyone else to keep up.\n\nAnd that’s where it got weird, because if a coworker questioned my plan or said, “Maybe we should slow down,” I’d snap at him way harder than made sense. Like instantly irritated, almost offended, as if they were getting in the way of something important. At the time I would’ve said they were just being negative or lazy, but now I can see my reaction was too much.\n\nSo… I don’t know. In the moment it felt like normal variation, just being driven. But when I compare it to how I usually am, it does seem more like a distinct episode or state or whatever you’d call it.\n\nDoctor: When you're not feeling fired up, do you think you're a different person compared to when you are in one of those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… honestly, I do feel like a different version of myself. Not like I’m literally a different person, but the way I think and react is different enough that when I look back on it, it almost doesn’t feel like my normal self.\n\nWhen I’m not fired up, I can usually hear someone out, or at least I’m not so quick to take it personally. Like if a coworker says, “Maybe we should slow down,” or questions part of a plan, I might get annoyed, but I can usually pause and think about it.\n\nBut during that park project, before I crashed, I felt so fired up and almost… bigger than myself, I guess. Like I had this certainty that I knew what needed to happen and everyone else just needed to get on board. And if someone questioned me, I’d snap at him way harder than made sense. It wasn’t just irritation — it felt like they were blocking something important, and I’d react like it was some huge offense.\n\nThen later, when I’m back to normal, I’ll think, “Why did I talk to him like that?” Because that’s not really how I want to be. So yeah, I guess it does feel like I become a more intense, impatient version of myself.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling fired up, do you think it's something that happens to you, or is it just your normal self being more motivated?\n\nPatient: I think at the time it feels like my normal self, just more motivated, like I’m finally getting things done the way I should. I don’t experience it as, “Oh, something is happening to me,” while I’m in it. It feels more like I’m right, I’m clear, I have energy, and everyone else is dragging their feet.\n\nBut looking back, especially with the park project, it really doesn’t feel like just normal motivation. Before I crashed, I felt fired up in this intense way, almost bigger than myself, like I had this strong sense that I knew exactly what the plan should be. And if a coworker questioned that plan or told me we should slow down, I’d snap at him way harder than made sense.\n\nThat’s the part that makes me think maybe it’s not just me being motivated. Because normal motivated me might be focused or a little impatient, but I don’t usually react like someone is personally attacking me just because they suggest we take a step back. In that state, it feels like they’re blocking something important, and I get irritated fast. Then later I can see it was too much. So… in the moment it feels like me, but afterward it feels like something kind of took over or pushed me past my normal self.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your periods of feeling fired up and then depressed are connected, or are they separate events?\n\nPatient: I think they’re connected, yeah. At least with the park pitch thing, it didn’t feel like two totally random events. The fired-up part started when I began trying to sort of remake myself for that pitch — like I was pushing myself into this bigger, more confident version of me — and that whole stretch went on for about two weeks.\n\nIt wasn’t like one good afternoon and then I got sad for unrelated reasons. It was more like I kept building and building in that overconfident, fired-up state, and then after about two weeks I crashed. So looking back, it feels like part of the same cycle, even though when I was in it, I didn’t really see it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or treated for bipolar disorder?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t ever been diagnosed with that or treated for it, at least not formally. I mean, nobody’s sat me down and said, “This is what you have,” or put me on some specific treatment for it.\n\nI’ve wondered about it, obviously, because of the way these ups and downs feel, but I don’t really know enough to say. I’ve mostly just thought of it as me having periods where I get really driven and then periods where I fall apart afterward, but I haven’t had an actual diagnosis or treatment plan for that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if I’d have called it “abnormal” while it was happening, because in the moment it felt like I was just really on top of things.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess what brought me in is I’ve been kind of… I don’t know, ramped up around this park project idea. It was more like once it got in my head, I couldn’t shut it off. I kept thinking of another person to call, another angle, another thing to price out, another message to send. I was walking around thinking, “Why hasn’t anyone done this already? I can make this happen.” But then if someone questioned me, like a coworker saying the plan didn’t make sense or telling me to slow down, I’d get irritated really fast. Like way too fast. During that stretch it felt like I was just going, going, going with the idea, like everything was connected to it. Then after those couple weeks it wasn’t the same anymore. It was like all that certainty and energy just dropped off.\n\nDoctor: After you crashed out of that fired-up, overly confident feeling, did you feel sad, empty, or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t want to say it was like I had some clear, named feeling right away, like “now I’m depressed.” It was more like the air went out of me, if that makes sense.\n\nAfter being so keyed up and convinced this whole park thing was going somewhere, it felt strange and kind of flat when that dropped off. But I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly. Empty maybe? I don’t know. I mostly remember feeling kind of confused and worn down, like I’d been running on something and then suddenly it wasn’t there anymore.\n\nI guess I noticed I wasn’t excited anymore, and that felt bad in its own way. But if you’re asking whether I was crying all the time or had this obvious heavy depression feeling, I don’t know that I can say that for sure. It was more like… quiet, and uncomfortable, and I didn’t really know what to do with myself after being so wrapped up in it.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a period of time after the park project idea wasn't exciting anymore where you felt consistently sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I think if I’m being honest, it wasn’t just a couple bad days after the park thing stopped feeling exciting. That crash kind of landed on top of something that had already been hanging around for a long time.\n\nI’d say the depressed feeling has been over me for about a year now, like this weight in the background, but after the park project excitement dropped off, it got worse. This latest crash is probably the worst it’s gotten. It’s hard to separate it neatly, because I keep thinking, “Was I already depressed before that, and then I just got distracted by the project?” But yeah, after it wasn’t exciting anymore, I felt consistently low, and it didn’t really lift.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt abnormally happy or irritable and had increased energy, like with the park project, but without any clear reason or goal?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think if there was no clear reason or goal… because with the park project, there was definitely a thing I was aiming at, you know? It wasn’t random in my mind. It felt like I had this purpose and I was building everything around it.\n\nBut the feeling itself, before I crashed, did feel kind of abnormal for me. I was fired up in this way that was more than just being motivated. It was like I felt almost bigger than myself, like I had more force behind me than I usually do. And it wasn’t just happy exactly—it could turn sharp really fast.\n\nLike if a coworker questioned part of my plan, or even just said something like maybe I should slow down or think it through more, I’d snap at him way harder than made sense. In the moment it felt like they were blocking something important, but looking back, the reaction was out of proportion. I’d get irritated fast, like, “Why can’t you see this?” even if they were probably just trying to be reasonable.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’ve had that same thing with no reason at all, but I have had that fired-up, almost inflated feeling, and the irritability came with it pretty strongly.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt this abnormally happy or irritable with increased energy?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, probably more than once, though the park project is the one that stands out the clearest because it got so wrapped up in an actual plan.\n\nBut I’ve had that same kind of feeling before, where I’m not just in a good mood — it’s more like I’m fired up, like I’ve got this extra push in me, almost like I’m bigger than myself for a while. And when I’m in it, it doesn’t feel strange at first. It feels like, “Oh, this is what I’m supposed to be doing,” or “I finally have energy.” But then the way I react to people is what makes me look back and think it wasn’t normal.\n\nLike if someone at work questioned me, or said I should slow down, or just didn’t immediately get on board with what I was saying, I’d snap at them way harder than made sense. In my head at the time, it felt justified, like they were in the way or not understanding something obvious. But afterward I could see I was being too intense. So yes, I’d say there’s been more than one period like that, with that fired-up energy and irritability.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these periods of feeling fired up and then depressed seem to happen in a repeating pattern over time?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to look back and see if there’s, like, a rhythm to it, but it’s hard for me to tell. When I’m in the fired-up part, I don’t really think, “Oh, this is one of those times again.” It feels more like I’ve finally gotten myself together or I finally have energy and direction. Then when I drop, it feels like everything just catches up with me, and I start questioning whether I was ever doing okay in the first place.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve noticed it’s happened more than once, the getting really intense and then later feeling low, but I don’t know if I can say it’s a repeating pattern in a clean way. It’s not like I could point to a calendar and say, “Every few months this happens.” It’s more messy than that. I only really see it looking backward, and even then I’m not sure if I’m connecting dots that are actually there or just trying to make sense of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these periods of feeling fired up and then depressed happen in a cyclical pattern over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it cyclical exactly. I mean, I get what you’re asking, like whether it comes around in some kind of cycle or pattern, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed it that clearly.\n\nIt’s more like… when I look back, I can see there have been times where I was really fired up, really intense, and then later I’d be down. But in the moment it doesn’t feel like, “Here we go again, this is the cycle starting.” It feels like whatever is happening right then is just because of my life or my work or something I’m focused on.\n\nAnd then after I’m low, I start trying to piece it together, but it’s messy. I don’t know if I’m seeing an actual pattern or if I’m just trying to make sense of it after the fact. So I guess my answer is… maybe, but I haven’t been able to track it well enough to say yes for sure.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel like you were able to function normally or even better than normal during those periods when you were fired up, despite not sleeping much or needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… at the time I actually thought I was functioning better than normal. Like, that was part of what made it hard to see it as a problem, because I wasn’t just lying around or acting useless. I was getting things done — or at least I felt like I was.\n\nDuring that park project, I’d be up before daylight, already loading tools, thinking through what needed to happen that day, sketching out planting beds, looking up prices for native plants, trying to figure out what would work where. And then I’d go do a full day outside, physical work, and instead of coming home wiped out like I normally would, I still felt like I had to keep going. Like there was more to plan, more to fix, more to push forward.\n\nSo yeah, in my head it felt like I didn’t need as much sleep because I had all this momentum. I remember thinking, “This is good, I’m finally productive.” But looking back, it was kind of like I couldn’t shut it off. Even after working all day, I didn’t feel done. I felt driven, like stopping would mean I was wasting something.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of feeling fired up or abnormally happy that lasted for less than a week?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, less than a week… that’s hard for me to pin down, because I don’t usually mark the start and end of it clearly when it’s happening. It’s not like I wake up and think, “Okay, this is day one of being fired up.” It’s more gradual, or at least it feels that way to me.\n\nThere have been times where I felt really energized or unusually good for a few days, I think, but I don’t know if I can say for sure how long they lasted. Sometimes I only notice afterward that I was acting different or running on more energy than usual. And by then the days kind of blur together, especially if I was busy.\n\nSo… maybe? But I don’t feel confident giving you a clean yes or no. I’d probably need help figuring out what counts and how to track it, because my memory of the timing isn’t very exact.\n\nDoctor: When you feel fired up like you did with the park project, do you think of it as a distinct episode or just a part of your normal variation in mood?\n\nPatient: I guess at the time I treated it like it was just me being “on,” like maybe this was my normal motivated side finally showing up. I didn’t think, “This is a distinct thing happening to me.” I thought I was just locked in.\n\nBut looking back at the park project, it does feel separate from my usual mood. Before I crashed, I wasn’t just in a good mood or working hard. I felt fired up in this really intense way, almost like I was bigger than myself, like I could see exactly how everything should be done and I just needed everyone else to keep up.\n\nAnd that’s where it got weird, because if a coworker questioned my plan or said, “Maybe we should slow down,” I’d snap at him way harder than made sense. Like instantly irritated, almost offended, as if they were getting in the way of something important. At the time I would’ve said they were just being negative or lazy, but now I can see my reaction was too much.\n\nSo… I don’t know. In the moment it felt like normal variation, just being driven. But when I compare it to how I usually am, it does seem more like a distinct episode or state or whatever you’d call it.\n\nDoctor: When you're not feeling fired up, do you think you're a different person compared to when you are in one of those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… honestly, I do feel like a different version of myself. Not like I’m literally a different person, but the way I think and react is different enough that when I look back on it, it almost doesn’t feel like my normal self.\n\nWhen I’m not fired up, I can usually hear someone out, or at least I’m not so quick to take it personally. Like if a coworker says, “Maybe we should slow down,” or questions part of a plan, I might get annoyed, but I can usually pause and think about it.\n\nBut during that park project, before I crashed, I felt so fired up and almost… bigger than myself, I guess. Like I had this certainty that I knew what needed to happen and everyone else just needed to get on board. And if someone questioned me, I’d snap at him way harder than made sense. It wasn’t just irritation — it felt like they were blocking something important, and I’d react like it was some huge offense.\n\nThen later, when I’m back to normal, I’ll think, “Why did I talk to him like that?” Because that’s not really how I want to be. So yeah, I guess it does feel like I become a more intense, impatient version of myself.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling fired up, do you think it's something that happens to you, or is it just your normal self being more motivated?\n\nPatient: I think at the time it feels like my normal self, just more motivated, like I’m finally getting things done the way I should. I don’t experience it as, “Oh, something is happening to me,” while I’m in it. It feels more like I’m right, I’m clear, I have energy, and everyone else is dragging their feet.\n\nBut looking back, especially with the park project, it really doesn’t feel like just normal motivation. Before I crashed, I felt fired up in this intense way, almost bigger than myself, like I had this strong sense that I knew exactly what the plan should be. And if a coworker questioned that plan or told me we should slow down, I’d snap at him way harder than made sense.\n\nThat’s the part that makes me think maybe it’s not just me being motivated. So… in the moment it feels like me, but afterward it feels like something kind of took over or pushed me past my normal self.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your periods of feeling fired up and then depressed are connected, or are they separate events?\n\nPatient: I think they’re connected, yeah. At least with the park pitch thing, it didn’t feel like two totally random events. The fired-up part started when I began trying to sort of remake myself for that pitch — like I was pushing myself into this bigger, more confident version of me — and that whole stretch went on for about two weeks.\n\nIt wasn’t like one good afternoon and then I got sad for unrelated reasons. It was more like I kept building and building in that overconfident, fired-up state, and then after about two weeks I crashed. So looking back, it feels like part of the same cycle, even though when I was in it, I didn’t really see it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or treated for bipolar disorder?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t ever been diagnosed with that or treated for it, at least not formally. I mean, nobody’s sat me down and said, “This is what you have,” or put me on some specific treatment for it.\n\nI’ve wondered about it, obviously, because of the way these ups and downs feel, but I don’t really know enough to say. I’ve mostly just thought of it as me having periods where I get really driven and then periods where I fall apart afterward, but I haven’t had an actual diagnosis or treatment plan for that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of abnormally elevated or irritable mood that lasted for at least one week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it does feel like a separate state, I guess. Not like I black out or become a totally different person, but it’s definitely not my normal way of being.

Before I crashed, I remember feeling really fired up, like I was almost bigger than myself. I had this sense that I could see everything clearly and that other people just weren’t keeping up. And because of that, if a coworker questioned my plan or even just suggested I slow down, I’d snap at him way harder than made sense. In the moment it felt justified, like he was blocking something important, but looking back, it was out of proportion.

So yeah, it feels distinct. It’s still me, but it’s like me turned way up, and not always in a good way.

I’d get irritated fast, like, “Why can’t you see this?” even if they were probably just trying to be reasonable.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’ve had that same thing with no reason at all, but I have had that fired-up, almost inflated feeling, and the irritability came with it pretty strongly.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt this abnormally happy or irritable with increased energy?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, probably more than once, though the park project is the one that stands out the clearest because it got so wrapped up in an actual plan.\n\nBut I’ve had that same kind of feeling before, where I’m not just in a good mood — it’s more like I’m fired up, like I’ve got this extra push in me, almost like I’m bigger than myself for a while. In my head at the time, it felt justified, like they were in the way or not understanding something obvious. But afterward I could see I was being too intense. I’ve been trying to look back and see if there’s, like, a rhythm to it, but it’s hard for me to tell. When I’m in the fired-up part, I don’t really think, “Oh, this is one of those times again.” It feels more like I’ve finally gotten myself together or I finally have energy and direction. Then when I drop, it feels like everything just catches up with me, and I start questioning whether I was ever doing okay in the first place.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve noticed it’s happened more than once, the getting really intense and then later feeling low, but I don’t know if I can say it’s a repeating pattern in a clean way. But in the moment it doesn’t feel like, “Here we go again, this is the cycle starting.” It feels like whatever is happening right then is just because of my life or my work or something I’m focused on.\n\nAnd then after I’m low, I start trying to piece it together, but it’s messy. Like instantly irritated, almost offended, as if they were getting in the way of something important. But when I compare it to how I usually am, it does seem more like a distinct episode or state or whatever you’d call it.\n\nDoctor: When you're not feeling fired up, do you think you're a different person compared to when you are in one of those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… honestly, I do feel like a different version of myself. Not like I’m literally a different person, but the way I think and react is different enough that when I look back on it, it almost doesn’t feel like my normal self.\n\nWhen I’m not fired up, I can usually hear someone out, or at least I’m not so quick to take it personally. Like if a coworker says, “Maybe we should slow down,” or questions part of a plan, I might get annoyed, but I can usually pause and think about it.\n\nBut during that park project, before I crashed, I felt so fired up and almost… bigger than myself, I guess. Like I had this certainty that I knew what needed to happen and everyone else just needed to get on board. And if someone questioned me, I’d snap at him way harder than made sense. It wasn’t just irritation — it felt like they were blocking something important, and I’d react like it was some huge offense.\n\nThen later, when I’m back to normal, I’ll think, “Why did I talk to him like that?” Because that’s not really how I want to be. So yeah, I guess it does feel like I become a more intense, impatient version of myself.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling fired up, do you think it's something that happens to you, or is it just your normal self being more motivated?\n\nPatient: I think at the time it feels like my normal self, just more motivated, like I’m finally getting things done the way I should. Before I crashed, I felt fired up in this intense way, almost bigger than myself, like I had this strong sense that I knew exactly what the plan should be. Because normal motivated me might be focused or a little impatient, but I don’t usually react like someone is personally attacking me just because they suggest we take a step back. In that state, it feels like they’re blocking something important, and I get irritated fast. Then later I can see it was too much. So… in the moment it feels like me, but afterward it feels like something kind of took over or pushed me past my normal self.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your periods of feeling fired up and then depressed are connected, or are they separate events?\n\nPatient: I think they’re connected, yeah. It wasn’t random in my mind. It felt like I had this purpose and I was building everything around it.\n\nBut the feeling itself, before I crashed, did feel kind of abnormal for me. I was fired up in this way that was more than just being motivated. It was like I felt almost bigger than myself, like I had more force behind me than I usually do. And it wasn’t just happy exactly—it could turn sharp really fast.\n\nLike if a coworker questioned part of my plan, or even just said something like maybe I should slow down or think it through more, I’d snap at him way harder than made sense. In the moment it felt like they were blocking something important, but looking back, the reaction was out of proportion. I’d get irritated fast, like, “Why can’t you see this?” even if they were probably just trying to be reasonable.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’ve had that same thing with no reason at all, but I have had that fired-up, almost inflated feeling, and the irritability came with it pretty strongly.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt this abnormally happy or irritable with increased energy?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, probably more than once, though the park project is the one that stands out the clearest because it got so wrapped up in an actual plan.\n\nBut I’ve had that same kind of feeling before, where I’m not just in a good mood — it’s more like I’m fired up, like I’ve got this extra push in me, almost like I’m bigger than myself for a while. And when I’m in it, it doesn’t feel strange at first. It feels like, “Oh, this is what I’m supposed to be doing,” or “I finally have energy.” But then the way I react to people is what makes me look back and think it wasn’t normal.\n\nLike if someone at work questioned me, or said I should slow down, or just didn’t immediately get on board with what I was saying, I’d snap at them way harder than made sense. In my head at the time, it felt justified, like they were in the way or not understanding something obvious. But afterward I could see I was being too intense. So yes, I’d say there’s been more than one period like that, with that fired-up energy and irritability.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these periods of feeling fired up and then depressed seem to happen in a repeating pattern over time?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to look back and see if there’s, like, a rhythm to it, but it’s hard for me to tell. When I’m in the fired-up part, I don’t really think, “Oh, this is one of those times again.” It feels more like I’ve finally gotten myself together or I finally have energy and direction. Then when I drop, it feels like everything just catches up with me, and I start questioning whether I was ever doing okay in the first place.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve noticed it’s happened more than once, the getting really intense and then later feeling low, but I don’t know if I can say it’s a repeating pattern in a clean way. It’s not like I could point to a calendar and say, “Every few months this happens.” It’s more messy than that. I only really see it looking backward, and even then I’m not sure if I’m connecting dots that are actually there or just trying to make sense of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these periods of feeling fired up and then depressed happen in a cyclical pattern over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it cyclical exactly. I mean, I get what you’re asking, like whether it comes around in some kind of cycle or pattern, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed it that clearly.\n\nIt’s more like… when I look back, I can see there have been times where I was really fired up, really intense, and then later I’d be down. But in the moment it doesn’t feel like, “Here we go again, this is the cycle starting.” It feels like whatever is happening right then is just because of my life or my work or something I’m focused on.\n\nAnd then after I’m low, I start trying to piece it together, but it’s messy. I don’t know if I’m seeing an actual pattern or if I’m just trying to make sense of it after the fact. So I guess my answer is… maybe, but I haven’t been able to track it well enough to say yes for sure.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel like you were able to function normally or even better than normal during those periods when you were fired up, despite not sleeping much or needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… at the time I actually thought I was functioning better than normal. Like, that was part of what made it hard to see it as a problem, because I wasn’t just lying around or acting useless. I was getting things done — or at least I felt like I was.\n\nDuring that park project, I’d be up before daylight, already loading tools, thinking through what needed to happen that day, sketching out planting beds, looking up prices for native plants, trying to figure out what would work where. And then I’d go do a full day outside, physical work, and instead of coming home wiped out like I normally would, I still felt like I had to keep going. Like there was more to plan, more to fix, more to push forward.\n\nSo yeah, in my head it felt like I didn’t need as much sleep because I had all this momentum. I remember thinking, “This is good, I’m finally productive.” But looking back, it was kind of like I couldn’t shut it off. Even after working all day, I didn’t feel done. I felt driven, like stopping would mean I was wasting something.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of feeling fired up or abnormally happy that lasted for less than a week?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, less than a week… that’s hard for me to pin down, because I don’t usually mark the start and end of it clearly when it’s happening. It’s not like I wake up and think, “Okay, this is day one of being fired up.” It’s more gradual, or at least it feels that way to me.\n\nThere have been times where I felt really energized or unusually good for a few days, I think, but I don’t know if I can say for sure how long they lasted. Sometimes I only notice afterward that I was acting different or running on more energy than usual. And by then the days kind of blur together, especially if I was busy.\n\nSo… maybe? But I don’t feel confident giving you a clean yes or no. I’d probably need help figuring out what counts and how to track it, because my memory of the timing isn’t very exact.\n\nDoctor: When you feel fired up like you did with the park project, do you think of it as a distinct episode or just a part of your normal variation in mood?\n\nPatient: I guess at the time I treated it like it was just me being “on,” like maybe this was my normal motivated side finally showing up. I didn’t think, “This is a distinct thing happening to me.” I thought I was just locked in.\n\nBut looking back at the park project, it does feel separate from my usual mood. Before I crashed, I wasn’t just in a good mood or working hard. I felt fired up in this really intense way, almost like I was bigger than myself, like I could see exactly how everything should be done and I just needed everyone else to keep up.\n\nAnd that’s where it got weird, because if a coworker questioned my plan or said, “Maybe we should slow down,” I’d snap at him way harder than made sense. Like instantly irritated, almost offended, as if they were getting in the way of something important. At the time I would’ve said they were just being negative or lazy, but now I can see my reaction was too much.\n\nSo… I don’t know. In the moment it felt like normal variation, just being driven. But when I compare it to how I usually am, it does seem more like a distinct episode or state or whatever you’d call it.\n\nDoctor: When you're not feeling fired up, do you think you're a different person compared to when you are in one of those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… honestly, I do feel like a different version of myself. Not like I’m literally a different person, but the way I think and react is different enough that when I look back on it, it almost doesn’t feel like my normal self.\n\nWhen I’m not fired up, I can usually hear someone out, or at least I’m not so quick to take it personally. Like if a coworker says, “Maybe we should slow down,” or questions part of a plan, I might get annoyed, but I can usually pause and think about it.\n\nBut during that park project, before I crashed, I felt so fired up and almost… bigger than myself, I guess. Like I had this certainty that I knew what needed to happen and everyone else just needed to get on board. And if someone questioned me, I’d snap at him way harder than made sense. It wasn’t just irritation — it felt like they were blocking something important, and I’d react like it was some huge offense.\n\nThen later, when I’m back to normal, I’ll think, “Why did I talk to him like that?” Because that’s not really how I want to be. So yeah, I guess it does feel like I become a more intense, impatient version of myself.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling fired up, do you think it's something that happens to you, or is it just your normal self being more motivated?\n\nPatient: I think at the time it feels like my normal self, just more motivated, like I’m finally getting things done the way I should. I don’t experience it as, “Oh, something is happening to me,” while I’m in it. Before I crashed, I felt fired up in this intense way, almost bigger than myself, like I had this strong sense that I knew exactly what the plan should be. In that state, it feels like they’re blocking something important, and I get irritated fast. Then later I can see it was too much.
